========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… maybe sometimes? Like at work I’ll hesitate before saying something because I don’t want to sound stupid, especially if the journeymen are talking fast and I’m still trying to catch up. But I’m not sure if that’s “rejection” exactly.

It’s more like… I’ll overthink it, and then the moment passes. Like there was this time with a pipe threader where I thought something was off, but then I started wondering if I was just being annoying, and then someone else noticed it anyway. So I guess I do hold back sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s because I’m afraid people will judge me or just because I don’t trust myself.

Yeah, my sleep’s been kind of all over the place.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Revved up is probably the right word.

It was like I had this charge in my body that wouldn’t shut off. I’d do a whole day working on pipes, and instead of coming home and crashing like a normal person, I’d go to evening class, then come back and reorganize all my tools, clean my apartment, and sometimes start drawing out systems or layouts like it was suddenly the most important thing.

And I mean, my tool bag does get messy, so part of me was like, “Well, this is useful,” but it was more than that. I just couldn’t really sit down.

Yeah, weirdly, I did. Or at least I thought I did.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t notice it as much at first, because in class we’re always kind of talking through problems or at the shop people are explaining stuff anyway.

Lately I either can’t fall asleep, or I wake up before my alarm and feel like I didn’t actually sleep at all.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t notice it as much at first, because in class we’re always kind of talking through problems or at the shop people are explaining stuff anyway.\n\nBut I’d start explaining some idea and then just keep going. Like I’d be talking about a layout or how something could be done, and then I’d realize I was talking over somebody, or they were trying to get back to work and I was still going, barely stopping to breathe.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because at the time I felt like I was just being clear or helpful, but looking back, yeah, it was more than usual.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that wired period last?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of hard to separate it into, like, one clean stretch of days. The whole pattern’s been going on for about five months, I guess—since I started the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.\n\nThat’s when everything kind of ramped up. I thought I was just motivated because it was a good opportunity, and the instructors were talking about greywater systems and efficiency stuff, which is actually pretty interesting, but… yeah, it’s been months, not just a couple days.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that wired period, did you feel unusually confident in yourself or your abilities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I honestly thought I could design something better than the instructors and the senior guys, like I was about to change how every building in Miami handled water if people would just listen.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I did. At the time it didn’t feel like “overconfident,” it felt like I was just finally seeing what everyone else was missing.

Like, I honestly thought I could design systems better than the instructors or the senior guys on site. I’d be sitting there thinking, if people would just listen to me, we could change how every building in Miami handles water. Which sounds… a lot, saying it now.

I got really caught up in that whole idea, like greywater and reuse and all that, and I was sure I had it figured out better than people who’ve been doing this way longer than me.

Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say “trouble” like serious trouble, but I definitely did stuff that wasn’t normal for me.

Lately I either can’t fall asleep, or I wake up before my alarm and feel like I didn’t actually sleep at all.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t notice it as much at first, because in class we’re always kind of talking through problems or at the shop people are explaining stuff anyway.\n\nBut I’d start explaining some idea and then just keep going. Like I’d be talking about a layout or how something could be done, and then I’d realize I was talking over somebody, or they were trying to get back to work and I was still going, barely stopping to breathe.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because at the time I felt like I was just being clear or helpful, but looking back, yeah, it was more than usual.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that wired period last?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of hard to separate it into, like, one clean stretch of days. The whole pattern’s been going on for about five months, I guess—since I started the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.\n\nThat’s when everything kind of ramped up. I thought I was just motivated because it was a good opportunity, and the instructors were talking about greywater systems and efficiency stuff, which is actually pretty interesting, but… yeah, it’s been months, not just a couple days.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, did you feel unusually confident in yourself or your abilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. At the time it didn’t feel like “overconfident,” it felt like I was just finally seeing what everyone else was missing.\n\nLike, I honestly thought I could design systems better than the instructors or the senior guys on site. I’d be sitting there thinking, if people would just listen to me, we could change how every building in Miami handles water. Which sounds… a lot, saying it now.\n\nI got really caught up in that whole idea, like greywater and reuse and all that, and I was sure I had it figured out better than people who’ve been doing this way longer than me.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, did you do anything that got you into trouble or was clearly unusual for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say “trouble” like serious trouble, but I definitely did stuff that wasn’t normal for me.\n\nI signed myself up to help a bunch of classmates with their projects, and then I was volunteering for extra design sketches too, like anytime someone mentioned an idea I’d be like, “Oh, I can draw that up.” And I started messaging old contacts about possible side projects, even people I hadn’t talked to in a while.\n\nAt the time it felt productive, like I was networking or whatever, but then I’d look at my week and it was like… there was no way the hours actually fit. Part of me knew that, but I kept saying yes anyway.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, did other people notice you were acting differently than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, people noticed. In class especially, or at the shop, I’d start explaining some idea I had and then I’d just… keep going. Like way past what the question needed.\n\nI could see people kind of trying to jump in, or the instructor trying to move on, or the guys needing to get back to work, and I’d still be talking over them, barely stopping to breathe. And sometimes it was about the project, but then I’d end up on, like, how the whole system should be redesigned, or this thing I saw in a building downtown, and I’d realize later nobody had asked for all that."
